From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF34.8, F34.9, F39, F48.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I came in because... well, at my church, I keep doing these odd things lately. Like at the potluck. We always have a potluck on the first Sunday, and Martha always brings her green bean casserole. She uses those little crispy onions on top, you know the ones in the can? I used to buy them at the Piggly Wiggly when they were on sale, back when my husband was still working. \n\nBut anyway, I was standing right there by the potluck table, and I was just holding the serving spoon up in the air, and I just wasn't moving at all. Just froze there holding it. And then later I was supposed to be helping, but I just kept folding the exact same stack of paper napkins over and over again. People were standing in line waiting on me to move, but I just kept folding them... they make those napkins so thin nowadays, not like they used to. You can barely wipe your mouth without them tearing to pieces. My mother used to have proper cloth ones with little embroidered daisies on the corners... \n\nWhat was I saying? Oh, yes, so I came in because folks were getting worried about me doing things like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any unusual thoughts or perceptions, like hearing voices that others don’t hear or believing something strongly that others might not agree with?\n\nPatient: Voices? Oh, no, Lord no. I don't hear anything like that. My hearing isn't even what it used to be, to tell you the truth. \n\nThough I will say, I can hear Mrs. Higgins' little dog yapping next door at all hours. It's one of those little scruffy terrier things. She got it for her grandson, but you know, young people today, they never come visit. They're always looking down at those little telephones. My own grandson, Jimmy, when he was little, he didn't need any of that. He used to sit right on my braided rug and play with these wooden blocks I found at a yard sale over on Elm Street. The lady having the sale, oh, she had the most beautiful blue hydrangeas I ever did see in her front yard. Just huge blooms. You have to put coffee grounds in the dirt to get them that pretty blue color, you know. My Aunt Betty taught me that back in... oh, it must have been 1968. She always saved her Folgers cans for her garden. \n\nBut... what was the question? Oh, voices. No, no, just my own regular thoughts. Nothing strange like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, sad, or have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy lately?\n\nPatient: Sad? Well, no, I wouldn't say I've been feeling sad. I still like doing my regular things just fine. I always make sure to sit down and watch my programs in the morning. The Price is Right comes on, though I'll tell you, it just hasn't been the exact same since Bob Barker left. He was such a handsome man, and he always used to tell folks at the end to get their pets fixed. \n\nMy neighbor down the street, Shirley, she used to have this big orange tomcat that she never got fixed, and he would just wander all over the neighborhood making a racket. He used to get right into my flower beds and dig up my marigolds. I tried putting cayenne pepper in the dirt because I read in a magazine that it keeps the cats away. I used to get the Reader's Digest delivered right to the house every single month. They always had those lovely little jokes in the back... Laughter is the Best Medicine, I think they called that section. My husband used to read them out loud to me while I was at the stove fixing supper. Usually I'd just make a meatloaf or fry up some pork chops. You really can't get a good thick cut of pork at the grocer anymore, though, it all cooks up so tough and dry... \n\nI'm sorry, doctor, what did you ask me? Was it about Shirley's cat?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts have been slower than usual or that your body feels physically slowed down, like you're moving through molasses?\n\nPatient: Molasses? Oh, no, I don't feel like that at all. I get around just fine for my age. Though speaking of molasses, I always used to buy that Grandma's brand, you know the glass jar with the yellow label? I'd use it to make my gingerbread men every December. My Jimmy just loved those when he was little. We'd always go pick out a Christmas tree over at the lot by the old Miller's hardware store right after I finished baking. \n\nThey tore that hardware store down... oh, it must be fifteen years ago now. They put up one of those dollar stores instead. Seems like they're putting a dollar store on every single corner these days. I went into one last week just to get some Dawn dish soap, and the girl at the register didn't even look up from her little telephone to say hello. People just don't teach manners anymore. When I was a girl, if you didn't say 'yes ma'am' and 'no sir', my daddy would send you right out to the yard to cut a switch from the willow tree. We had this big, beautiful weeping willow right by the creek out back of the house... we used to catch tadpoles in that creek in the springtime in these little glass mason jars. \n\nWait now, what were we talking about? Did you want my recipe for the gingerbread?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any unusual ideas or beliefs that other people might not share, like feeling like someone is watching you or controlling your thoughts?\n\nPatient: Watching me? Oh, heavens no. I don't think anyone would be interested in watching an old woman like me. Though I will say, I used to love watching people when I'd take the bus downtown to go to the big department store. They had this lunch counter right there on the first floor where you could get a sandwich and a cup of coffee for just a few coins. \n\nI always liked to sit near the window and just look out at everybody walking by. The ladies used to dress up so nice to go shopping back then, always wearing a nice hat and gloves. You never see ladies wearing gloves anymore, unless it's snowing out. My mother had this beautiful pair of white kid leather gloves she only brought out for Easter Sunday. She kept them in a special cedar box in her dresser drawer, right next to this little bottle of rosewater perfume. It smelled just like the roses we had growing up the side of our front porch. We had a porch swing that my daddy built himself, painted it bright white every spring. We'd sit out there in the evenings and listen to the crickets... \n\nI'm sorry, doctor. What did you ask me just now? Did you want to know about the bus route?\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt like nothing could make you happy or you couldn’t enjoy things, even the things you usually like?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I can always find a little something to enjoy, even if it's just my morning cup of Sanka. I like to sit by the kitchen window and watch the blue jays out in the yard. We used to have this beautiful concrete birdbath out there... my husband brought it home from a yard sale one Saturday. He just loved a good yard sale. He was always bringing home old radios and clocks to tinker with. He had a whole workbench out in the garage just covered in little wires and tubes. \n\nHe could fix just about anything, you know. He fixed my toaster once when the lever wouldn't pop up anymore. It was one of those heavy, shiny chrome ones, made real sturdy. You can't put those thick slices of bread in them, though, or they just get stuck and burn. I just hate the smell of burnt toast, it gets right into the curtains and you have to take them all down and wash them. I used to wash every curtain in the house come springtime, and I'd hang them out on the clothesline to dry. They smelled so fresh from the wind blowing through them... \n\nI'm sorry, doctor. What were we talking about? Did you ask me about the laundry?\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the past few weeks, have you ever felt down or lost interest in things for a day or more, even if it didn’t seem like a big deal at the time?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really felt down for a whole day or anything like that. I keep myself busy enough, you know. I was just organizing my recipe box yesterday. It's one of those old tin ones with the little painted daisies on the front. I got it at a church bazaar back in... oh, it must have been 1982. Pastor Miller's wife, Susan, she painted all those tins herself. She was so talented with a paintbrush. She used to paint these beautiful little landscapes on river rocks and sell them as paperweights. \n\nI bought one with a little red barn on it for my husband's desk. He worked down at the insurance company for thirty years, had this big heavy oak desk right in the middle of the floor. He used to keep a jar of hard candies on it for the secretaries. Those little strawberry ones with the soft center? They wrapped them in that shiny wrapper that looked like a real strawberry. I always liked the butterscotch ones better myself. They come in that gold wrapper that crinkles so loud when you open it. You can't open one in church without everybody turning around to look at you...\n\nOh, dear, I'm sorry. Did you ask me about my recipes?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more tired or low on energy lately, even when you're doing things you normally find easy?\n\nPatient: Tired? Oh, heavens no, I've got plenty of get-up-and-go. I still walk all the way down the long driveway to get the mail every single morning. Our mailman, Mr. Henderson, he's usually just pulling up in that little white truck with the steering wheel on the wrong side. He always gives a little honk when he sees me. He's been on our route for... oh, it must be fifteen years now. \n\nHe brought me a package once that my sister sent all the way from Scotland. It was a big tin of shortbread cookies, the real buttery kind that just melt right on your tongue. The tin had this beautiful red and green plaid pattern all over it. My grandmother was Scottish, you know, she came over on a boat when she was just a little girl. She used to make these wonderful oatcakes on a big heavy cast-iron griddle. That griddle weighed an absolute ton, I swear. She had to use two hands just to lift it off the stove. She was a strong woman, though, grew up working on a dairy farm up in Wisconsin, milking cows before the sun even came up... \n\nOh, my goodness, I'm so sorry. I lost my train of thought again. What did you ask me just a minute ago? Did you want to know if I was expecting a letter?\n\nDoctor: Would you say you’ve been feeling down or sad most of the time, even if it’s not very intense?\n\nPatient: Sad? No, I wouldn't say I've been sad. I keep a fairly even keel, mostly. I like to sit out on the porch when the weather is nice, just watching the cars go by on the main road. There was a big yellow moving truck that went past yesterday, one of those really long ones. It reminded me of when my neighbors, the Millers, moved away to Florida. They packed up their whole house in just two days. \n\nMrs. Miller had this beautiful collection of porcelain teacups, she wrapped every single one in newspaper so they wouldn't break. She had one with little violets painted all around the rim, with a tiny gold handle. I always thought gold handles looked so elegant, but you can't put them in the microwave, you know. My daughter ruined a perfectly good plate that way, it just sparked and cracked right down the middle. It sounded like a firecracker going off in the kitchen. We used to light sparklers on the Fourth of July when the kids were little, they'd run around the yard drawing circles in the dark...\n\nOh, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I just start talking and the words run away from me. What was your question, doctor? Did you ask about the neighbors?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, even if you're not feeling very sad, you've had a general sense that things are less enjoyable or that life feels a bit heavier than usual?\n\nPatient: Heavier? No, I wouldn't say that. I still enjoy my days just fine. I was actually just watering my African violets this morning, and they always bring a smile to my face. They're blooming so beautifully right now, these deep purple petals with the little yellow centers. My niece, Sarah, bought me the first one for Mother's Day years ago. She got it in this little terra cotta pot that had a lace ribbon tied around it. \n\nSarah always was so good with crafts. She used to make those macaroni necklaces when she was just a little thing in grade school. She'd paint the noodles gold and string them on red yarn. I'd wear them to the grocery store just to make her proud. The grocery store down on Main Street used to have a real butcher counter, you know, where they'd wrap your meat in that thick white paper and tie it with string. The butcher, Mr. Franks, he always gave the kids a free slice of bologna. It had that little red string around the edge you had to peel off. You don't see bologna like that much anymore, mostly it just comes in those plastic packages you have to tear open with your teeth...\n\nOh, goodness me. I've wandered off again, haven't I? My mind just goes for a walk. What were we just talking about? Did you ask if I needed to go to the store?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble concentrating or thinking clearly lately, like it's harder to follow conversations or keep track of what you're doing?\n\nPatient: Trouble concentrating? Well, I suppose I have noticed something like that. When the ladies at the church ask me things, like about the blankets we're knitting or when choir practice is, I really do mean to answer them straight. But my words, they just run off on me. I'll start out fine, and then suddenly I'm talking about canning green beans, and then the Sunday hymns, and then my rose bushes. \n\nI have these beautiful pink climbing roses on the trellis out back. My husband built that trellis for me out of some old cedar wood he got from the lumber yard. The lumber yard used to be right next to the train tracks, and you could hear the whistle blow every single day right at noon. We used to set our kitchen clock by that whistle. My grandfather had a pocket watch, a heavy silver one that ticked so loud you could hear it clear across the parlor. He kept it on a little chain tucked right into his vest pocket... \n\nOh, heavens. I'm doing it right now, aren't I? Just talking a blue streak. That's what happens, you see, and then folks just look at me like they can't find what I'm saying at all. Did you ask me what time it was, doctor?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself slowing down or doing things more slowly than usual, especially when you’re trying to get something done around the house or when you’re with other people?\n\nPatient: Slowing down? No, I wouldn't say I'm moving any slower. I get my chores done just about the same as always. I was just sweeping the kitchen floor this morning after breakfast. I still use that old straw broom I bought at the hardware store downtown. It sweeps so much better than those plastic ones they sell at the big supermarkets nowadays. \n\nThe hardware store closed down a few years back, which is a real shame. Mr. Henderson ran it for fifty years, I reckon. He always wore those striped denim overalls with the little pockets on the front for his carpenter pencils. My brother used to wear overalls exactly like that when he worked on the tractor out in the fields. It was a big green John Deere, loud as anything. You could hear him plowing the lower field from a mile away, just a big rumble in the dirt. We used to walk down and take him sweet iced tea in a big glass mason jar when the sun got too hot. Those jars were so heavy when they were full, we'd wrap a checkered dish towel around them so our hands wouldn't freeze from the condensation... \n\nOh, goodness. I've gone and lost the thread again, haven't I? My mouth just runs away without me. What was it you asked? Did you ask if I wanted some tea?\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Have you noticed yourself talking more quickly or more than usual, or feeling like your thoughts are racing ahead of you?\n\nPatient: Talking more quickly? No, I don't think I'm talking any faster than I usually do. My thoughts don't feel like they're racing, just... wandering, I suppose. Like when I'm out in the garden. I was out there yesterday pulling weeds around the tomatoes. They're getting so tall now, I had to tie them up to those wooden stakes my neighbor gave me. He's such a nice man, Mr. Abernathy. He brings over his extra zucchini every summer in this little wicker basket. \n\nI used to have a wicker basket just like it that I'd take to the farmer's market on Saturdays. They had the sweetest peaches there, the kind where the juice just runs right down your chin. We'd buy a whole peck and make preserves in the big copper kettle my mother left me. That kettle must be a hundred years old by now. It takes a lot of elbow grease to keep it shining, using salt and vinegar on a rag. Vinegar is good for so many things, you know, cleaning windows and taking the smell out of the laundry...\n\nOh, dear me. I've done it again, haven't I? Just talking and talking about nothing at all. What were we saying, doctor? Did you ask about my garden?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself losing interest in any of your usual activities, even for a short time, lately?\n\nPatient: Losing interest? Oh, no, I wouldn't say that. I still enjoy my regular things just fine. I was just working on my quilting this morning, actually. I'm piecing together a double wedding ring pattern for my grand-niece. She's getting married next spring down by the lake. Spring is such a beautiful time for a wedding, especially when the dogwood trees are blooming. We had a giant dogwood right by the front porch when I was growing up. My older brother used to climb up in it and hide from our mother when it was time to pluck the chickens. He'd sit up there with his pocket knife, just whittling away at little sticks of pine. He carved a tiny little bear once and gave it to the school teacher, Miss Lottie. She always wore these big pearl earrings that looked like little snowballs. She told us they came from oysters all the way out in the ocean, which just sounded like magic to us kids... \n\nOh, my stars. I've gone and talked myself into a circle again, haven't I? I just can't seem to keep my thoughts on a straight path today. What did you ask me, doctor? Were we talking about the wedding?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that things you usually enjoy, like your quilting or watching your favorite shows, don’t give you as much pleasure as they used to?\n\nPatient: Less pleasure? Oh, no, I still enjoy them just fine. I was watching my programs just last night, in fact. I like those game shows they play on the classic channel. We used to watch them on a little black and white television set we had in the living room years and years ago. It had those rabbit ear antennas on top, you know? My husband used to roll up little balls of aluminum foil and stick them on the ends of the metal to try and make the picture come in clearer. Aluminum foil is getting so awfully expensive these days, isn't it? I went to buy a roll at the pharmacy just the other week, and it was nearly five dollars. Five dollars! I remember when you could get a whole loaf of bread and a gallon of milk for less than that. We used to get our milk delivered right to the front porch in glass bottles by a milkman named Charlie. He drove a white truck that just rattled over the roads, and he always left a little pat of butter on holidays... \n\nOh, heavens to Betsy. I've gone and wandered off again, haven't I? My mind just hops like a frog from one lily pad to the next today. What was it you were asking me? Well, yes, I suppose I do. Like when the ladies ask me about the blankets we're putting together or about choir practice. I really do mean to answer them straight, but my words just run off. I'll start talking, and suddenly I've gone from snapping green beans, to the hymns we're singing, all the way out to my rose bushes. \n\nThe roses are doing so well this year, especially the yellow ones by the back fence. My mother always said yellow roses were for friendship. She used to keep a little glass vase of them on the kitchen table right next to the salt and pepper shakers. Those shakers were shaped like little brown owls, so cute. We bought them at a roadside stand on our way down to the county fair one summer... oh, they had the biggest prize-winning pumpkins at that fair. One time a man grew a pumpkin so big they had to bring it in on a flatbed tractor, and they made pies out of it for the whole town... \n\nAnd then I look up at the ladies, and they're just staring at me like they can't find what I'm saying. It makes this awful racket sometimes in the winter. We had a radiator just like it in the old schoolhouse I went to. It was a little one-room place, and in January it would get so cold we'd all have to keep our coats on during spelling practice. \n\nI had this heavy wool coat with big wooden buttons. My aunt knitted it for me. She was a wonderful knitter, she could make a whole sweater in just a few days. She used to sit by the fire with her knitting needles just clicking away, making these beautiful red and green scarves for Christmas. We always had a real pine tree for Christmas, and we'd string popcorn and cranberries to put all around it. The birds would eat the popcorn right off the branches when we put the tree outside in January... \n\nOh, dear. I've gone and done it again. I have a big metal tin, the kind that used to have Danish butter cookies in it. The blue one, you know? My grandmother gave me that tin. She loved those cookies, especially the ones with the little sugar crystals on top. We would have them with tea on Sunday afternoons. She had this beautiful china tea set with little pink roses painted on the cups. \n\nPink roses are so delicate, aren't they? I tried growing pink ones once, but the beetles got to them terribly. You have to be so careful with garden pests. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he uses dish soap and water to keep the bugs off his tomato plants. He grows the biggest beefsteak tomatoes you've ever seen. He brought me one last August, and I made a whole sandwich out of just one slice. I put a little mayonnaise and black pepper on it... pepper always makes me sneeze if I'm not careful. I sneezed three times in a row at church last Sunday during the sermon. \n\nOh, my goodness. I'm just babbling away like a brook, aren't I? I completely lost the thread of what we were talking about. The lumber yard used to be right next to the train tracks, and you could hear the whistle blow every single day right at noon. We used to set our kitchen clock by that whistle. My grandfather had a pocket watch, a heavy silver one that ticked so loud you could hear it clear across the parlor. He kept it on a little chain tucked right into his vest pocket... \n\nOh, heavens. I'm doing it right now, aren't I? Just talking a blue streak. That's what happens, you see, and then folks just look at me like they can't find what I'm saying at all. Did you ask me what time it was, doctor?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself slowing down or doing things more slowly than usual, especially when you’re trying to get something done around the house or when you’re with other people?\n\nPatient: Slowing down? No, I wouldn't say I'm moving any slower. I get my chores done just about the same as always. I was just sweeping the kitchen floor this morning after breakfast. I still use that old straw broom I bought at the hardware store downtown. It sweeps so much better than those plastic ones they sell at the big supermarkets nowadays. \n\nThe hardware store closed down a few years back, which is a real shame. Mr. Henderson ran it for fifty years, I reckon. He always wore those striped denim overalls with the little pockets on the front for his carpenter pencils. My brother used to wear overalls exactly like that when he worked on the tractor out in the fields. It was a big green John Deere, loud as anything. You could hear him plowing the lower field from a mile away, just a big rumble in the dirt. We used to walk down and take him sweet iced tea in a big glass mason jar when the sun got too hot. Those jars were so heavy when they were full, we'd wrap a checkered dish towel around them so our hands wouldn't freeze from the condensation... \n\nOh, goodness. I've gone and lost the thread again, haven't I? My mouth just runs away without me. What was it you asked? Did you ask if I wanted some tea?\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Have you noticed yourself talking more quickly or more than usual, or feeling like your thoughts are racing ahead of you?\n\nPatient: Talking more quickly? No, I don't think I'm talking any faster than I usually do. My thoughts don't feel like they're racing, just... wandering, I suppose. Like when I'm out in the garden. I was out there yesterday pulling weeds around the tomatoes. They're getting so tall now, I had to tie them up to those wooden stakes my neighbor gave me. He's such a nice man, Mr. Abernathy. He brings over his extra zucchini every summer in this little wicker basket. \n\nI used to have a wicker basket just like it that I'd take to the farmer's market on Saturdays. They had the sweetest peaches there, the kind where the juice just runs right down your chin. We'd buy a whole peck and make preserves in the big copper kettle my mother left me. That kettle must be a hundred years old by now. It takes a lot of elbow grease to keep it shining, using salt and vinegar on a rag. Vinegar is good for so many things, you know, cleaning windows and taking the smell out of the laundry...\n\nOh, dear me. I've done it again, haven't I? Just talking and talking about nothing at all. What were we saying, doctor? Did you ask about my garden?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself losing interest in any of your usual activities, even for a short time, lately?\n\nPatient: Losing interest? Oh, no, I wouldn't say that. I still enjoy my regular things just fine. I was just working on my quilting this morning, actually. I'm piecing together a double wedding ring pattern for my grand-niece. She's getting married next spring down by the lake. Spring is such a beautiful time for a wedding, especially when the dogwood trees are blooming. We had a giant dogwood right by the front porch when I was growing up. My older brother used to climb up in it and hide from our mother when it was time to pluck the chickens. He'd sit up there with his pocket knife, just whittling away at little sticks of pine. He carved a tiny little bear once and gave it to the school teacher, Miss Lottie. She always wore these big pearl earrings that looked like little snowballs. She told us they came from oysters all the way out in the ocean, which just sounded like magic to us kids... \n\nOh, my stars. I've gone and talked myself into a circle again, haven't I? I just can't seem to keep my thoughts on a straight path today. What did you ask me, doctor? Were we talking about the wedding?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that things you usually enjoy, like your quilting or watching your favorite shows, don’t give you as much pleasure as they used to?\n\nPatient: Less pleasure? Oh, no, I still enjoy them just fine. I was watching my programs just last night, in fact. I like those game shows they play on the classic channel. We used to watch them on a little black and white television set we had in the living room years and years ago. It had those rabbit ear antennas on top, you know? My husband used to roll up little balls of aluminum foil and stick them on the ends of the metal to try and make the picture come in clearer. Aluminum foil is getting so awfully expensive these days, isn't it? I went to buy a roll at the pharmacy just the other week, and it was nearly five dollars. Five dollars! I remember when you could get a whole loaf of bread and a gallon of milk for less than that. We used to get our milk delivered right to the front porch in glass bottles by a milkman named Charlie. He drove a white truck that just rattled over the roads, and he always left a little pat of butter on holidays... \n\nOh, heavens to Betsy. I've gone and wandered off again, haven't I? My mind just hops like a frog from one lily pad to the next today. What was it you were asking me? Did you ask about the television?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that during conversations, your mind tends to drift or you have difficulty staying focused on the topic at hand?\n\nPatient: Drifting? Well, yes, I suppose I do. Like when the ladies ask me about the blankets we're putting together or about choir practice. I really do mean to answer them straight, but my words just run off. I'll start talking, and suddenly I've gone from snapping green beans, to the hymns we're singing, all the way out to my rose bushes. \n\nThe roses are doing so well this year, especially the yellow ones by the back fence. My mother always said yellow roses were for friendship. She used to keep a little glass vase of them on the kitchen table right next to the salt and pepper shakers. Those shakers were shaped like little brown owls, so cute. We bought them at a roadside stand on our way down to the county fair one summer... oh, they had the biggest prize-winning pumpkins at that fair. One time a man grew a pumpkin so big they had to bring it in on a flatbed tractor, and they made pies out of it for the whole town... \n\nAnd then I look up at the ladies, and they're just staring at me like they can't find what I'm saying. Oh, dear me. I'm doing it again, aren't I? Just floating right off the subject like a kite without a string. What were we talking about, doctor? Was it the blankets?\n\nDoctor: Have you found that your attention is easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant things, like getting distracted by small noises or objects when you're trying to focus on something?\n\nPatient: Distracted by little noises? Oh, no, I wouldn't say that. I don't really pay much mind to things like that when I'm busy. Well, unless it's the radiator clanking in the hallway, I suppose. It makes this awful racket sometimes in the winter. We had a radiator just like it in the old schoolhouse I went to. It was a little one-room place, and in January it would get so cold we'd all have to keep our coats on during spelling practice. \n\nI had this heavy wool coat with big wooden buttons. My aunt knitted it for me. She was a wonderful knitter, she could make a whole sweater in just a few days. She used to sit by the fire with her knitting needles just clicking away, making these beautiful red and green scarves for Christmas. We always had a real pine tree for Christmas, and we'd string popcorn and cranberries to put all around it. The birds would eat the popcorn right off the branches when we put the tree outside in January... \n\nOh, dear. I've gone and done it again. My mouth just runs like a river sometimes and leaves my brain behind on the bank. I'm so sorry. What did you ask me about? Was it the radiator?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you couldn’t get interested in anything, even the things you usually enjoy, for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Not interested in things? No, I don't think I've ever felt that way for days on end. I always find something to keep my hands busy. Even when I'm just sitting, I like to sort through my button tin. I have a big metal tin, the kind that used to have Danish butter cookies in it. The blue one, you know? My grandmother gave me that tin. She loved those cookies, especially the ones with the little sugar crystals on top. We would have them with tea on Sunday afternoons. She had this beautiful china tea set with little pink roses painted on the cups. \n\nPink roses are so delicate, aren't they? I tried growing pink ones once, but the beetles got to them terribly. You have to be so careful with garden pests. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he uses dish soap and water to keep the bugs off his tomato plants. He grows the biggest beefsteak tomatoes you've ever seen. He brought me one last August, and I made a whole sandwich out of just one slice. I put a little mayonnaise and black pepper on it... pepper always makes me sneeze if I'm not careful. I sneezed three times in a row at church last Sunday during the sermon. \n\nOh, my goodness. I'm just babbling away like a brook, aren't I? I completely lost the thread of what we were talking about. Did you ask me about my buttons?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Oh, right, you were asking about losing interest, not my buttons. I'm sorry, doctor. No, I don't think I ever just sit around feeling like I don't want to do anything. I always have to be keeping busy, even if it's just sweeping the front steps. 

I was out there sweeping just yesterday morning, actually, and this little orange stray cat came right up to me. I've been leaving a little saucer of milk out for him on the railing. My mother always told us you shouldn't give cats milk because it upsets their stomachs, but he just laps it right up. We used to get our milk delivered in glass bottles when I was a little girl. The milkman had this big white truck, and he'd come so early in the morning. In the wintertime, it would get so cold that the cream at the top of the bottle would freeze solid, and it would push the little paper cap right up in the air. It looked just like a little top hat on a snowman. We used to build snowmen in the front yard and use coal for the eyes. You don't see coal much anymore, but my father used to shovel it into the furnace down in the cellar... 

Oh, my stars. I've gone right off the rails again, haven't I? Just talking your ear off about the milkman. What was it we were trying to settle? Did you want to know about the sweeping?

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF34.8, F34.9, F39, F48.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I came in because... well, at my church, I keep doing these odd things lately. Like at the potluck. We always have a potluck on the first Sunday, and Martha always brings her green bean casserole. She uses those little crispy onions on top, you know the ones in the can? I used to buy them at the Piggly Wiggly when they were on sale, back when my husband was still working. \n\nBut anyway, I was standing right there by the potluck table, and I was just holding the serving spoon up in the air, and I just wasn't moving at all. Just froze there holding it. And then later I was supposed to be helping, but I just kept folding the exact same stack of paper napkins over and over again. People were standing in line waiting on me to move, but I just kept folding them... they make those napkins so thin nowadays, not like they used to. You can barely wipe your mouth without them tearing to pieces. My mother used to have proper cloth ones with little embroidered daisies on the corners... \n\nWhat was I saying? Oh, yes, so I came in because folks were getting worried about me doing things like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any unusual thoughts or perceptions, like hearing voices that others don’t hear or believing something strongly that others might not agree with?\n\nPatient: Voices? Oh, no, Lord no. I don't hear anything like that. My hearing isn't even what it used to be, to tell you the truth. \n\nThough I will say, I can hear Mrs. Higgins' little dog yapping next door at all hours. It's one of those little scruffy terrier things. She got it for her grandson, but you know, young people today, they never come visit. They're always looking down at those little telephones. My own grandson, Jimmy, when he was little, he didn't need any of that. He used to sit right on my braided rug and play with these wooden blocks I found at a yard sale over on Elm Street. The lady having the sale, oh, she had the most beautiful blue hydrangeas I ever did see in her front yard. Just huge blooms. You have to put coffee grounds in the dirt to get them that pretty blue color, you know. My Aunt Betty taught me that back in... oh, it must have been 1968. She always saved her Folgers cans for her garden. \n\nBut... what was the question? Oh, voices. No, no, just my own regular thoughts. Nothing strange like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, sad, or have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy lately?\n\nPatient: Sad? Well, no, I wouldn't say I've been feeling sad. I still like doing my regular things just fine. I always make sure to sit down and watch my programs in the morning. The Price is Right comes on, though I'll tell you, it just hasn't been the exact same since Bob Barker left. He was such a handsome man, and he always used to tell folks at the end to get their pets fixed. \n\nMy neighbor down the street, Shirley, she used to have this big orange tomcat that she never got fixed, and he would just wander all over the neighborhood making a racket. He used to get right into my flower beds and dig up my marigolds. I tried putting cayenne pepper in the dirt because I read in a magazine that it keeps the cats away. I used to get the Reader's Digest delivered right to the house every single month. They always had those lovely little jokes in the back... Laughter is the Best Medicine, I think they called that section. My husband used to read them out loud to me while I was at the stove fixing supper. Usually I'd just make a meatloaf or fry up some pork chops. You really can't get a good thick cut of pork at the grocer anymore, though, it all cooks up so tough and dry... \n\nI'm sorry, doctor, what did you ask me? Was it about Shirley's cat?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts have been slower than usual or that your body feels physically slowed down, like you're moving through molasses?\n\nPatient: Molasses? Oh, no, I don't feel like that at all. I get around just fine for my age. Though speaking of molasses, I always used to buy that Grandma's brand, you know the glass jar with the yellow label? I'd use it to make my gingerbread men every December. My Jimmy just loved those when he was little. We'd always go pick out a Christmas tree over at the lot by the old Miller's hardware store right after I finished baking. \n\nThey tore that hardware store down... oh, it must be fifteen years ago now. They put up one of those dollar stores instead. Seems like they're putting a dollar store on every single corner these days. I went into one last week just to get some Dawn dish soap, and the girl at the register didn't even look up from her little telephone to say hello. People just don't teach manners anymore. When I was a girl, if you didn't say 'yes ma'am' and 'no sir', my daddy would send you right out to the yard to cut a switch from the willow tree. We had this big, beautiful weeping willow right by the creek out back of the house... we used to catch tadpoles in that creek in the springtime in these little glass mason jars. \n\nWait now, what were we talking about? Did you want my recipe for the gingerbread?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any unusual ideas or beliefs that other people might not share, like feeling like someone is watching you or controlling your thoughts?\n\nPatient: Watching me? Oh, heavens no. I don't think anyone would be interested in watching an old woman like me. Though I will say, I used to love watching people when I'd take the bus downtown to go to the big department store. They had this lunch counter right there on the first floor where you could get a sandwich and a cup of coffee for just a few coins. \n\nI always liked to sit near the window and just look out at everybody walking by. The ladies used to dress up so nice to go shopping back then, always wearing a nice hat and gloves. You never see ladies wearing gloves anymore, unless it's snowing out. My mother had this beautiful pair of white kid leather gloves she only brought out for Easter Sunday. She kept them in a special cedar box in her dresser drawer, right next to this little bottle of rosewater perfume. It smelled just like the roses we had growing up the side of our front porch. We had a porch swing that my daddy built himself, painted it bright white every spring. We'd sit out there in the evenings and listen to the crickets... \n\nI'm sorry, doctor. What did you ask me just now? Did you want to know about the bus route?\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt like nothing could make you happy or you couldn’t enjoy things, even the things you usually like?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I can always find a little something to enjoy, even if it's just my morning cup of Sanka. I like to sit by the kitchen window and watch the blue jays out in the yard. We used to have this beautiful concrete birdbath out there... my husband brought it home from a yard sale one Saturday. He just loved a good yard sale. He was always bringing home old radios and clocks to tinker with. He had a whole workbench out in the garage just covered in little wires and tubes. \n\nHe could fix just about anything, you know. He fixed my toaster once when the lever wouldn't pop up anymore. It was one of those heavy, shiny chrome ones, made real sturdy. You can't put those thick slices of bread in them, though, or they just get stuck and burn. I just hate the smell of burnt toast, it gets right into the curtains and you have to take them all down and wash them. I used to wash every curtain in the house come springtime, and I'd hang them out on the clothesline to dry. They smelled so fresh from the wind blowing through them... \n\nI'm sorry, doctor. What were we talking about? Did you ask me about the laundry?\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the past few weeks, have you ever felt down or lost interest in things for a day or more, even if it didn’t seem like a big deal at the time?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really felt down for a whole day or anything like that. I keep myself busy enough, you know. I was just organizing my recipe box yesterday. It's one of those old tin ones with the little painted daisies on the front. I got it at a church bazaar back in... oh, it must have been 1982. Pastor Miller's wife, Susan, she painted all those tins herself. She was so talented with a paintbrush. She used to paint these beautiful little landscapes on river rocks and sell them as paperweights. \n\nI bought one with a little red barn on it for my husband's desk. He worked down at the insurance company for thirty years, had this big heavy oak desk right in the middle of the floor. He used to keep a jar of hard candies on it for the secretaries. Those little strawberry ones with the soft center? They wrapped them in that shiny wrapper that looked like a real strawberry. I always liked the butterscotch ones better myself. They come in that gold wrapper that crinkles so loud when you open it. You can't open one in church without everybody turning around to look at you...\n\nOh, dear, I'm sorry. Did you ask me about my recipes?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more tired or low on energy lately, even when you're doing things you normally find easy?\n\nPatient: Tired? Oh, heavens no, I've got plenty of get-up-and-go. I still walk all the way down the long driveway to get the mail every single morning. Our mailman, Mr. Henderson, he's usually just pulling up in that little white truck with the steering wheel on the wrong side. He always gives a little honk when he sees me. He's been on our route for... oh, it must be fifteen years now. \n\nHe brought me a package once that my sister sent all the way from Scotland. It was a big tin of shortbread cookies, the real buttery kind that just melt right on your tongue. The tin had this beautiful red and green plaid pattern all over it. My grandmother was Scottish, you know, she came over on a boat when she was just a little girl. She used to make these wonderful oatcakes on a big heavy cast-iron griddle. That griddle weighed an absolute ton, I swear. She had to use two hands just to lift it off the stove. She was a strong woman, though, grew up working on a dairy farm up in Wisconsin, milking cows before the sun even came up... \n\nOh, my goodness, I'm so sorry. I lost my train of thought again. What did you ask me just a minute ago? Did you want to know if I was expecting a letter?\n\nDoctor: Would you say you’ve been feeling down or sad most of the time, even if it’s not very intense?\n\nPatient: Sad? No, I wouldn't say I've been sad. I keep a fairly even keel, mostly. I like to sit out on the porch when the weather is nice, just watching the cars go by on the main road. There was a big yellow moving truck that went past yesterday, one of those really long ones. It reminded me of when my neighbors, the Millers, moved away to Florida. They packed up their whole house in just two days. \n\nMrs. Miller had this beautiful collection of porcelain teacups, she wrapped every single one in newspaper so they wouldn't break. She had one with little violets painted all around the rim, with a tiny gold handle. I always thought gold handles looked so elegant, but you can't put them in the microwave, you know. My daughter ruined a perfectly good plate that way, it just sparked and cracked right down the middle. It sounded like a firecracker going off in the kitchen. We used to light sparklers on the Fourth of July when the kids were little, they'd run around the yard drawing circles in the dark...\n\nOh, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I just start talking and the words run away from me. What was your question, doctor? No, I wouldn't say that. I still enjoy my days just fine. I was actually just watering my African violets this morning, and they always bring a smile to my face. They're blooming so beautifully right now, these deep purple petals with the little yellow centers. My niece, Sarah, bought me the first one for Mother's Day years ago. She got it in this little terra cotta pot that had a lace ribbon tied around it. \n\nSarah always was so good with crafts. She used to make those macaroni necklaces when she was just a little thing in grade school. She'd paint the noodles gold and string them on red yarn. I'd wear them to the grocery store just to make her proud. The grocery store down on Main Street used to have a real butcher counter, you know, where they'd wrap your meat in that thick white paper and tie it with string. The butcher, Mr. Franks, he always gave the kids a free slice of bologna. It had that little red string around the edge you had to peel off. You don't see bologna like that much anymore, mostly it just comes in those plastic packages you have to tear open with your teeth...\n\nOh, goodness me. I've wandered off again, haven't I? Well, I suppose I have noticed something like that. You could hear him plowing the lower field from a mile away, just a big rumble in the dirt. We used to walk down and take him sweet iced tea in a big glass mason jar when the sun got too hot. Those little strawberry ones with the soft center? They wrapped them in that shiny wrapper that looked like a real strawberry. I always liked the butterscotch ones better myself. They come in that gold wrapper that crinkles so loud when you open it. You can't open one in church without everybody turning around to look at you...\n\nOh, dear, I'm sorry. Did you ask me about my recipes?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more tired or low on energy lately, even when you're doing things you normally find easy?\n\nPatient: Tired? Oh, heavens no, I've got plenty of get-up-and-go. I still walk all the way down the long driveway to get the mail every single morning. Our mailman, Mr. Henderson, he's usually just pulling up in that little white truck with the steering wheel on the wrong side. He always gives a little honk when he sees me. He's been on our route for... oh, it must be fifteen years now. \n\nHe brought me a package once that my sister sent all the way from Scotland. It was a big tin of shortbread cookies, the real buttery kind that just melt right on your tongue. The tin had this beautiful red and green plaid pattern all over it. My grandmother was Scottish, you know, she came over on a boat when she was just a little girl. She used to make these wonderful oatcakes on a big heavy cast-iron griddle. That griddle weighed an absolute ton, I swear. She had to use two hands just to lift it off the stove. She was a strong woman, though, grew up working on a dairy farm up in Wisconsin, milking cows before the sun even came up... \n\nOh, my goodness, I'm so sorry. I lost my train of thought again. What did you ask me just a minute ago? Did you want to know if I was expecting a letter?\n\nDoctor: Would you say you’ve been feeling down or sad most of the time, even if it’s not very intense?\n\nPatient: Sad? No, I wouldn't say I've been sad. I keep a fairly even keel, mostly. I like to sit out on the porch when the weather is nice, just watching the cars go by on the main road. There was a big yellow moving truck that went past yesterday, one of those really long ones. It reminded me of when my neighbors, the Millers, moved away to Florida. They packed up their whole house in just two days. \n\nMrs. Miller had this beautiful collection of porcelain teacups, she wrapped every single one in newspaper so they wouldn't break. She had one with little violets painted all around the rim, with a tiny gold handle. I always thought gold handles looked so elegant, but you can't put them in the microwave, you know. My daughter ruined a perfectly good plate that way, it just sparked and cracked right down the middle. It sounded like a firecracker going off in the kitchen. We used to light sparklers on the Fourth of July when the kids were little, they'd run around the yard drawing circles in the dark...\n\nOh, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I just start talking and the words run away from me. What was your question, doctor? Did you ask about the neighbors?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, even if you're not feeling very sad, you've had a general sense that things are less enjoyable or that life feels a bit heavier than usual?\n\nPatient: Heavier? No, I wouldn't say that. I still enjoy my days just fine. I was actually just watering my African violets this morning, and they always bring a smile to my face. They're blooming so beautifully right now, these deep purple petals with the little yellow centers. My niece, Sarah, bought me the first one for Mother's Day years ago. She got it in this little terra cotta pot that had a lace ribbon tied around it. \n\nSarah always was so good with crafts. She used to make those macaroni necklaces when she was just a little thing in grade school. She'd paint the noodles gold and string them on red yarn. I'd wear them to the grocery store just to make her proud. The grocery store down on Main Street used to have a real butcher counter, you know, where they'd wrap your meat in that thick white paper and tie it with string. The butcher, Mr. Franks, he always gave the kids a free slice of bologna. It had that little red string around the edge you had to peel off. You don't see bologna like that much anymore, mostly it just comes in those plastic packages you have to tear open with your teeth...\n\nOh, goodness me. I've wandered off again, haven't I? My mind just goes for a walk. What were we just talking about? Did you ask if I needed to go to the store?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble concentrating or thinking clearly lately, like it's harder to follow conversations or keep track of what you're doing?\n\nPatient: Trouble concentrating? Well, I suppose I have noticed something like that. When the ladies at the church ask me things, like about the blankets we're knitting or when choir practice is, I really do mean to answer them straight. But my words, they just run off on me. I'll start out fine, and then suddenly I'm talking about canning green beans, and then the Sunday hymns, and then my rose bushes. \n\nI have these beautiful pink climbing roses on the trellis out back. My husband built that trellis for me out of some old cedar wood he got from the lumber yard. The lumber yard used to be right next to the train tracks, and you could hear the whistle blow every single day right at noon. We used to set our kitchen clock by that whistle. My grandfather had a pocket watch, a heavy silver one that ticked so loud you could hear it clear across the parlor. He kept it on a little chain tucked right into his vest pocket... \n\nOh, heavens. I'm doing it right now, aren't I? Just talking a blue streak. That's what happens, you see, and then folks just look at me like they can't find what I'm saying at all. Did you ask me what time it was, doctor?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself slowing down or doing things more slowly than usual, especially when you’re trying to get something done around the house or when you’re with other people?\n\nPatient: Slowing down? No, I wouldn't say I'm moving any slower. I get my chores done just about the same as always. I was just sweeping the kitchen floor this morning after breakfast. I still use that old straw broom I bought at the hardware store downtown. It sweeps so much better than those plastic ones they sell at the big supermarkets nowadays. \n\nThe hardware store closed down a few years back, which is a real shame. Mr. Henderson ran it for fifty years, I reckon. He always wore those striped denim overalls with the little pockets on the front for his carpenter pencils. My brother used to wear overalls exactly like that when he worked on the tractor out in the fields. It was a big green John Deere, loud as anything. You could hear him plowing the lower field from a mile away, just a big rumble in the dirt. We used to walk down and take him sweet iced tea in a big glass mason jar when the sun got too hot. Those jars were so heavy when they were full, we'd wrap a checkered dish towel around them so our hands wouldn't freeze from the condensation... \n\nOh, goodness. I've gone and lost the thread again, haven't I? My mouth just runs away without me. What was it you asked? Did you ask if I wanted some tea?\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Have you noticed yourself talking more quickly or more than usual, or feeling like your thoughts are racing ahead of you?\n\nPatient: Talking more quickly? No, I don't think I'm talking any faster than I usually do. My thoughts don't feel like they're racing, just... wandering, I suppose. Like when I'm out in the garden. I was out there yesterday pulling weeds around the tomatoes. They're getting so tall now, I had to tie them up to those wooden stakes my neighbor gave me. He's such a nice man, Mr. Abernathy. He brings over his extra zucchini every summer in this little wicker basket. \n\nI used to have a wicker basket just like it that I'd take to the farmer's market on Saturdays. They had the sweetest peaches there, the kind where the juice just runs right down your chin. We'd buy a whole peck and make preserves in the big copper kettle my mother left me. That kettle must be a hundred years old by now. It takes a lot of elbow grease to keep it shining, using salt and vinegar on a rag. Vinegar is good for so many things, you know, cleaning windows and taking the smell out of the laundry...\n\nOh, dear me. I've done it again, haven't I? Just talking and talking about nothing at all. What were we saying, doctor? Did you ask about my garden?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself losing interest in any of your usual activities, even for a short time, lately?\n\nPatient: Losing interest? Oh, no, I wouldn't say that. I still enjoy my regular things just fine. I was just working on my quilting this morning, actually. I'm piecing together a double wedding ring pattern for my grand-niece. She's getting married next spring down by the lake. Spring is such a beautiful time for a wedding, especially when the dogwood trees are blooming. We had a giant dogwood right by the front porch when I was growing up. My older brother used to climb up in it and hide from our mother when it was time to pluck the chickens. He'd sit up there with his pocket knife, just whittling away at little sticks of pine. He carved a tiny little bear once and gave it to the school teacher, Miss Lottie. She always wore these big pearl earrings that looked like little snowballs. She told us they came from oysters all the way out in the ocean, which just sounded like magic to us kids... \n\nOh, my stars. I've gone and talked myself into a circle again, haven't I? I just can't seem to keep my thoughts on a straight path today. What did you ask me, doctor? Were we talking about the wedding?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that things you usually enjoy, like your quilting or watching your favorite shows, don’t give you as much pleasure as they used to?\n\nPatient: Less pleasure? Oh, no, I still enjoy them just fine. I was watching my programs just last night, in fact. I like those game shows they play on the classic channel. We used to watch them on a little black and white television set we had in the living room years and years ago. It had those rabbit ear antennas on top, you know? My husband used to roll up little balls of aluminum foil and stick them on the ends of the metal to try and make the picture come in clearer. Aluminum foil is getting so awfully expensive these days, isn't it? I went to buy a roll at the pharmacy just the other week, and it was nearly five dollars. Five dollars! I remember when you could get a whole loaf of bread and a gallon of milk for less than that. We used to get our milk delivered right to the front porch in glass bottles by a milkman named Charlie. He drove a white truck that just rattled over the roads, and he always left a little pat of butter on holidays... \n\nOh, heavens to Betsy. I've gone and wandered off again, haven't I? My mind just hops like a frog from one lily pad to the next today. What was it you were asking me? Did you ask about the television?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that during conversations, your mind tends to drift or you have difficulty staying focused on the topic at hand?\n\nPatient: Drifting? Well, yes, I suppose I do. Like when the ladies ask me about the blankets we're putting together or about choir practice. I really do mean to answer them straight, but my words just run off. I'll start talking, and suddenly I've gone from snapping green beans, to the hymns we're singing, all the way out to my rose bushes. \n\nThe roses are doing so well this year, especially the yellow ones by the back fence. My mother always said yellow roses were for friendship. She used to keep a little glass vase of them on the kitchen table right next to the salt and pepper shakers. Those shakers were shaped like little brown owls, so cute. We bought them at a roadside stand on our way down to the county fair one summer... oh, they had the biggest prize-winning pumpkins at that fair. One time a man grew a pumpkin so big they had to bring it in on a flatbed tractor, and they made pies out of it for the whole town... \n\nAnd then I look up at the ladies, and they're just staring at me like they can't find what I'm saying. Oh, dear me. I'm doing it again, aren't I? Just floating right off the subject like a kite without a string. What were we talking about, doctor? Was it the blankets?\n\nDoctor: Have you found that your attention is easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant things, like getting distracted by small noises or objects when you're trying to focus on something?\n\nPatient: Distracted by little noises? Oh, no, I wouldn't say that. I don't really pay much mind to things like that when I'm busy. Well, unless it's the radiator clanking in the hallway, I suppose. It makes this awful racket sometimes in the winter. We had a radiator just like it in the old schoolhouse I went to. It was a little one-room place, and in January it would get so cold we'd all have to keep our coats on during spelling practice. \n\nI had this heavy wool coat with big wooden buttons. My aunt knitted it for me. She was a wonderful knitter, she could make a whole sweater in just a few days. She used to sit by the fire with her knitting needles just clicking away, making these beautiful red and green scarves for Christmas. We always had a real pine tree for Christmas, and we'd string popcorn and cranberries to put all around it. The birds would eat the popcorn right off the branches when we put the tree outside in January... \n\nOh, dear. I've gone and done it again. My mouth just runs like a river sometimes and leaves my brain behind on the bank. I'm so sorry. What did you ask me about? Was it the radiator?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you couldn’t get interested in anything, even the things you usually enjoy, for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Not interested in things? No, I don't think I've ever felt that way for days on end. I always find something to keep my hands busy. Even when I'm just sitting, I like to sort through my button tin. I have a big metal tin, the kind that used to have Danish butter cookies in it. The blue one, you know? My grandmother gave me that tin. She loved those cookies, especially the ones with the little sugar crystals on top. We would have them with tea on Sunday afternoons. She had this beautiful china tea set with little pink roses painted on the cups. \n\nPink roses are so delicate, aren't they? I tried growing pink ones once, but the beetles got to them terribly. You have to be so careful with garden pests. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he uses dish soap and water to keep the bugs off his tomato plants. He grows the biggest beefsteak tomatoes you've ever seen. He brought me one last August, and I made a whole sandwich out of just one slice. I put a little mayonnaise and black pepper on it... pepper always makes me sneeze if I'm not careful. I sneezed three times in a row at church last Sunday during the sermon. \n\nOh, my goodness. I'm just babbling away like a brook, aren't I? I completely lost the thread of what we were talking about. Did you ask me about my buttons?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you couldn’t get interested in anything, even the things you usually enjoy, for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Oh, right, you were asking about losing interest, not my buttons. I'm sorry, doctor. No, I don't think I ever just sit around feeling like I don't want to do anything. I always have to be keeping busy, even if it's just sweeping the front steps. \n\nI was out there sweeping just yesterday morning, actually, and this little orange stray cat came right up to me. I've been leaving a little saucer of milk out for him on the railing. My mother always told us you shouldn't give cats milk because it upsets their stomachs, but he just laps it right up. We used to get our milk delivered in glass bottles when I was a little girl. The milkman had this big white truck, and he'd come so early in the morning. In the wintertime, it would get so cold that the cream at the top of the bottle would freeze solid, and it would push the little paper cap right up in the air. It looked just like a little top hat on a snowman. We used to build snowmen in the front yard and use coal for the eyes. You don't see coal much anymore, but my father used to shovel it into the furnace down in the cellar... \n\nOh, my stars. I've gone right off the rails again, haven't I? Just talking your ear off about the milkman. What was it we were trying to settle? Did you want to know about the sweeping?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not feeling enjoyment with others? No, I wouldn't say that. I always enjoy seeing my family and the folks at church. I was just at church this past Sunday, actually. Pastor Miller gave such a lovely sermon about forgiveness. He always wears these brightly colored ties, and this week he had one with little yellow ducks on it. It reminded me of when I used to take my children to the park to feed the ducks. We'd save all our stale bread crusts in a paper bag. 

You know, the baker down on Main Street used to give us extra bread if we went right before closing. He was a sweet man, Mr. Rossi. He always smelled like yeast and cinnamon. He made the most wonderful cinnamon rolls, with that thick white icing dripping down the sides. I tried to make icing like that once, but it came out too runny. I think I added too much milk. You have to be so precise with baking. It's not like making a stew where you can just throw a little bit of this and a little bit of that into the pot. I make a very good beef stew, with big chunks of potatoes and carrots...

Oh, goodness gracious. There I go again, wandering all over the map. I am so sorry, doctor. I start talking and I just forget where I was going. What were we talking about? Was it the ducks?
